Asia EM Equity Strategy | Asia Pacific

# Alpha, Beta, Thematic Biweekly – Navigating Higher Rates, Elections and Geopolitics

Asia and EM equities are adjusting to a higher growth, rates and USD outlook, with rates correlations deteriorating in places but resilient in Japan, Taiwan and India. We also highlight APAC Aerospace and Defense exposures and the Korea post-election outlook. Market dynamics inside.

## Key Takeaways

Market Views: Japan and India remain top DM and EM picks, alongside OWs on Mexico, Poland, Greece and Singapore.

Sector/Style Views: We like Semis/Equipment into an AI boom and hardware recovery, and Industrials in India and Mexico. Momentum reversal is ongoing.

Key Themes: We like AI Enablers and Adopters, Japanese Corporate Reform, and select China Global Expansion and Korea Value opportunities.

Flows and Positioning: EM and China have seen outflows recently.

Top of the Agenda: Asia/EM equities are seeing a short-term correction and divergence open up in reaction to a stronger US growth outlook, shallower ratecutting profile and bullish USD trends (see our latest US and Asia macro forecasts). Japan and Taiwan remain well placed on US revenue exposure and correlations with end-2024 Fed expectations, while other markets have deteriorated (notably Australia, Brazil and Indonesia). Amid ongoing geopolitical risks in a Multipolar World transition, we also highlight a list of APAC Aerospace and Defense stocks.

## Top of the Agenda

Daniel Blake, Kristal Ji

This fortnight we tackle:

• US Macro Resilience – Too Much of a Good Thing?

• Korean Post-Elections - Focus on Self-Driven Reforms

• Indian Elections Underway – Long Bull Market Intact

## US Macro Resilience – Too Much of a Good Thing?

Following strong migration-related growth and labour market indicators, our US Economics Team has substantially lifted its GDP forecasts for 2024 and 2025. Recent bumpy inflation readings have not been entirely surprising given the progress made to date, and our team still sees a pipeline of disinflation coming through core services and shelter, with core PCE expected to edge down to 2.6% by 4Q24 and 2.1% by 4Q25. However, the team has pushed back timing of its first cut forecast from June to July, while expecting two additional cuts in November and December as well as cuts every meeting in 1H25, taking rates down to 3.625% by mid-2025. For the full profile and backdrop, see US Economics: Outlook Update: Near-Term Inflation Against a Positive Supply Shock (14 Apr 2024).

Factoring in this new outlook, our Asia economics team sees a later and shallower ratecutting cycle path for Asia-ex-China central banks. And in Japan, our economists now expect the BoJ to deliver another hike in January 2025, which would see the policy rate move up to 0.5% (following an increase this July to 25bp). See Asia Economics: The Viewpoint: An Even Shallower Rate Cut Path (15 Apr 2024) for more details.

Exhibit 3: We now expect a shallower rate cut cycle in Asia-ex-China-ex-Japan
![](images/a594d50ee575b1f62ac4e2384bcbae5dc72e428e1a7d840043af6473b5649cb7.jpg)
Source: Haver, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

How does this outlook for a stronger-for-longer US economy affect Asia and EM equities? At an aggregate level, we think the net effect is positive at the margin, although much of the good news was discounted in the 10% rally in the three months from January 17 to April 10. The new profile shows the US economy remaining a key engine of global growth, likely continuing the restocking cycle seen in parts of the hardware/OEM/auto supply chains, whilst also sustaining the capex programs that we have seen in key secular growth and de-risking segments, such as AI/data centers, semiconductors and renewables.

Exploring this growth sensitivity further, we note that Taiwan, Japan, Mexico and South Korea have the most direct North America revenue exposure and will likely benefit from firmer operating expectations into 2025 ( Exhibit 4 ). For Japan, a higher-for-longer rate US rate profile has the added impact of keeping the JPY at exceptionally weak levels, sustaining the upgrade cycle for export-oriented companies, notwithstanding the risk of FX intervention.

## But Higher Rates Likely to Weigh on EM FX Translation and Delay any Rate-sensitive Rotation

The cloudy side of the silver lining is that we see the timing of global monetary policy easing delayed. In our view, Asia/EM economies and equity markets have largely adjusted to the policy rates reached in 2023, making the key risk whether the Fed needs to go "higher", not just whether they stay at policy rates "longer".

Exhibit 6: EM and Japan correlations with Dec-24 Fed Funds pricing – Remains positive, as the growth impulse is outweighing the impact of discount rates, although correlations have been dropping especially for Japan given fears of potential FX intervention/reversa

Rolling 2-Month Daily Correlation against Dec 2024 Fed Fund Rate
![](images/d3156144fea38de846c65d1c4c3440776f2a3d9de1b9883945375ae6184cfd4d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research; Data as of April 12, 2024.

Thus the impacts of this repricing have been contained to the most interest-sensitive sectors rather than aggregate valuations. MSCI AC Asia Pacific Financials have outperformed ( Exhibit 7 ), while MSCI AC Asia Pacific Real Estate Index has been negatively affected by higher rate expectations and will likely see further underperformance if rates hold at current levels into 2025 ( Exhibit 8 ).

Exhibit 7: Futures-Implied Dec-24 Fed Fund Rate vs MSCI AC Asia Pacific Financials Index Relative Performance – Financials outperform with higher rates
![](images/d8c55dd3472335b822698bad384322468fbceba338051a38dbcda5222c0ef163.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Factset, Morgan Stanley Research, Data as of April 12, 2024

Exhibit 8: Futures-Implied Dec-24 Fed Fund Rate vs. MSCI AC Asia Pacific Real Estate Index Relative Performance - Real estate tends to underperform with higher rates
![](images/f42bef07008e47277677ad479ad5efa85520fe6e858f5f9e7f2c07684a106cb6.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Factset, Morgan Stanley Research, Data as of April 12, 2024

There may also be some further adjustment for markets that have traded up in expectation of policy rate cuts, such as Australia, where our economists remain far more cautious on the timing of an RBA easing cycle. Interest-rate correlations with Dec-24 Fed expectations have also deteriorated in places and turned negative in some markets – with the sharpest reversals in Brazil, Indonesia and Australia, where central banks and FX markets are closely watching the policy space afforded by the Fed outlook.

Exhibit 9: Change in market correlations with Dec-24 Fed Funds pricing – Korea has turned positive from negative, Turkey, Japan, Taiwan and Poland remain positively correlated with US rates; Hong Kong and China remain negatively correlated, and the rest see flipping patterns YTD vs. 2021 onward
![](images/4df9a9ebd68fde58e8c17a5dd3e64f8c3db9e449faeba6dc3ae0a63387b79e76.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research; Data as of April 12, 2024.

We are playing close attention to Indian market dynamics, which have remained quite resilient to date, consistent with our thesis that macro-stability has become a key driver of the bull market and a marked difference with prior cycles. Meanwhile, Chinese equities have also seen improving US rate correlations (from the most negative previously) as the growth cycle stabilises in the short run.

The other key channel of impact is through Asia and EM currencies, given both indices are reported in USD while seeing a majority of revenues generated in local currency. Our colleagues in EM macro strategy expect ongoing USD support versus low-yield FX counterparts (see Global EM Strategist: Carry On, 15 Apr 2024).

Exhibit 10: USD is a key driver of the relative performance of EM vs. DM equities

Source: FactSet, Datastream and Morgan Stanley Research Data as of April 15, 2024

![](images/1f0d3dee457488ca8d0b191e835100f876c6ce6a6528aebf0946fa696e03dc86.jpg)

## Bear Case is for More Fed Hikes and Hard Landing Risk

Overall, we think the larger risk to Asia and EM equities comes through a more inflationary and hawkish scenario, where the Fed is forced to recommence rate hikes – ultimately bearing the risk of engineering a hard landing to bring inflation back to target. In this scenario, we could see emerging signs of a negative rate correlation and FX stress broaden, further undercutting an already weak earnings cycle. We would also likely see a pivot in leadership away from markets with high US revenue exposure, such as Taiwan and Japan, toward more domestically oriented and resilient late-cycle markets, such as Emerging ASEAN and potentially China/HK.

## Multipolar World Dynamics Supporting Aerospace & Defence Returns

Global developments continue to reinforce our theme of a transition to a Multipolar World, as explored in Thematics: Practical Guide to a Multipolar World (13 Jun 2023) and Thematics: Seeking Security in the Multipolar World (23 Apr 2023). Measures of geopolitical risk remain elevated and recent events in the Middle East highlight the trend toward reinvestment in core aspects of national and economic security.

Exhibit 12: Geopolitical Risk Index (Caldara and Iacoviello) - Remains elevated, particularly vs. late 1990s and early 2010s

![](images/4384254981cfec5fc940a6bb02b0b34b2006bff42a9caca4b745d2782316e496.jpg)
Source: Caldara, Dario and Matteo Iacoviello (2022), “Measuring Geopolitical Risk,” American Economic Review, April, 112(4), pp.1194-1225. See https://www.matteoiacoviello.com/gpr.htm

To better track performance and operating trends for listed companies in the Aerospace and Defense sectors, we have identified a universe of securities using the following approaches:

1. Companies with GICS industry or BICS industry name classified as "Aerospace & Defense".

2. Companies with more than 5% of revenue from FactSet RBICS sub-sector “Aerospace & Defense Manufacturing”

3. Companies' business descriptions with Aerospace & Defense-related key words found.

4. Top companies in the annual procurement list quoted by Japan’s Ministry of Defense.

5. The defense contractor companies designated by Korea’s Ministry of Trade, Industry and Energy.

On a market-cap weighted basis, Asia Pacific Aerospace & Defense companies have outperformed the broader benchmark by a cumulative 206% since January 2014. Full constituents are provided in the Appendix and are available on request.

Exhibit 13: Asia Pacific Aerospace & Defense Stocks – Sustained outperformance in transition to Multipolar World

Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research; Market-capitalisation weighted, USD denominated price returns shown

## Korean Post-Elections – Focus on Self-Driven Reforms

The opposition Democratic Party of Korea gained a strong majority in the 2024 Parliamentary Elections, making the political environment more challenging over the remaining Presidential term. We think the corporate value-up program will continue, but some incentivization measures requiring legislative approval may face difficulty and therefore the path ahead requires greater scrutiny and emphasis on those taking initiative.

Our Korea Strategy Team recommends waiting for the dust to settle, before looking to buy into select financials, auto and discretionary names. See Korea Strategy: Liberals Take Supermajority (10 Apr 2024) and S. Korea Strategy and Economics: Extra Election Focus due to Corporate Reform (27 Mar 2024) for more details.

From the regional side, we hold an equal-weight stance on Korea in APxJ/EM and think that corporate execution remains key. Within Korea, we maintain our long-held preference for the IT and AI theme through Focus List inclusions, SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics, but we recently added Hyundai Motor for mid-cycle exposure and self-driven corporate

reform (see here for more details).

## Indian Elections Underway – Long Bull Market Intact

We think there are still legs to the India's bull market, with sustainable earnings power driven by a strong corporate capex and credit cycle that may bring further upside to earnings. Our India Equity Strategy Team sees a multiyear build-up in domestic savings into equities, together with improving quality of life of households and young demographics. Multipolar world dynamics are also bringing fresh orders to India as well as foreign portfolio flows. The team recommend several themes under Consumer, Energy, Financials, Industrials and Services - see India Equity Strategy: This Bull Market's Next Leg (14 Apr 2024) for more details.

At the same time, India General Elections are around the corner. We revisit India Equity Strategy: One Billion Voters: Will They Please the Market? (3 Sep 2023) and see policy continuity to be the major driving factor to market reactions. Historically, the market will trade optimistically into the election and the performance post election normally depends on policy directions and could be volatile.

We believe the market will still position and price in an election result that comes with policy continuity, although there are other macro forces that will come into play (a potentially later US rates cutting cycle and higher growth expectation, volatility in crude oil prices and shocks to inflation numbers, etc.). We iterate our Overweight stance on India in our market allocation framework with a strong earnings upcycle, continuous buildup of equity savings, stable macro outlook and young demographics.

## Flows and Positioning

## Kristal Ji

• Aggregate flows (EPFR, to April 10, 2024): EM and China recorded outflows, and active long-only US and EU funds were still selling Chinese equities ( Exhibit 49 ).

• Flows by market (EPFR, to April 10, 2024): Relative to AUM, Taiwan, India and Czech Republic recorded the largest inflows within EM, while Brazil , UAE and Greece recorded the largest outflows. ( Exhibit 48 ).

• Japan flows (JPX, to April 5, 2024): Japan foreign inflows have broadly come back since the end of October 2023, having net bought US\$7.4bn YTD 2024. Retailers have turned to net buyers YTD in aggregate after two weeks of meaningful inflows, and net bought US\$1.2bn YTD 2024 ( Exhibit 50 and Exhibit 51 ).

• Asia/EM Positioning (EPFR, to February 2024): India underweight in GEM funds allocation has been widening given managers have not yet caught up with the rising index weight. China is seeing dropping benchmark weight and GEM funds managers' allocation, with 100bp underweight vs. benchmark. Japan underweight in global long-only equity funds widened to 0.54% as of end-February, though we continue to expect Japan to eventually come to overweight ( Exhibit 53 ).

## Appendix - APAC Aerospace & Defence Stock List

We have identified a universe of listed securities across Asia Pacific markets with Aerospace & Defense exposure. Our approach is not exhaustive and may capture businesses with only a minority exposure to the sector. We utilised a combination of the following screens:

1. Companies with GICS Industry or BICS Industry Name classified as "Aerospace & Defense".

2. Companies with more than 5% of revenue from FactSet RBICS sub-sector “Aerospace and Defense Manufacturing”.

3. Companies' Business Descriptions with Aerospace & Defense-related key words found.

4. Japan's Ministry of Defense – Top companies in the annual procurement list.

5. Korea's Ministry of Trade, Industry and Energy – Companies designated as defense contractors.

## STOCK RATINGS

Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell. Investors should carefully read the definitions of all ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone. In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice. An investor's decision to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

## Analyst Stock Ratings

Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.

## Analyst Industry Views

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
